Graph weights and ordering chains

Copied Q&A · three input arguments and an internal ready-chain result

QnA · What does chain bookkeeping initialize, and why are there still three inputs? 
│  ├─
initialize weights={} and CPU/per-device ordering-chain bookkeeping ↗ graph.py:879
│  │  ├─
_has_chain_input=False; _DeviceChainMap(self) starts empty and retains G1 ↗ graph.py:880 ↗ graph.py:126
│  │  ├─
_add_op_generated(_mo.ChainCreateOp, result=ChainType(), inputs=[]) inserts an internal ready token ↗ graph.py:887
│  │  │  ├─
OpBuilder(Block._from_cmlir(_current_block).end) → construct, verify, wrap result ↗ graph.py:1257 ↗ graph.py:1277
│  │  │  └─
_always_ready_chain=%ch0; _update_chain(%ch0) sets device_chains[cpu:0]=%ch0 ↗ graph.py:894 ↗ graph.py:1064
│  │  └─
test B0's last argument: %v has TensorType, not ChainType → _has_chain_input stays False ↗ graph.py:896 ↗ graph.py:900
│  │
OUT: three user inputs plus an internal chain OP RESULT; no fourth input block argument ↗ graph.py:888 ↗ graph.py:900   => explain in deep , no edits allowed.

These lines give G1 a starting point for ordering operations. G1 already has one body block, B0, containing three input arguments: Q, K, and V. The constructor now adds bookkeeping and creates %ch0, a symbolic dependency value inside that block. Constructor source

The names B0, T0, and %ch0 below are teaching labels for the underlying MLIR objects.

self._weights = {} starts a cache of graph weights.

This dictionary associates a weight’s name with its Python Weight object and the symbolic tensor representing that weight in the graph.

For example, a graph containing a learned projection could eventually have:

G1._weights = {
    "projection.weight":
        _GraphWeight(
            weight = Python Weight object,
            value  = TensorValue wrapping %projection_weight
        )
}

Reusing that same weight can reuse the existing symbolic value. This bookkeeping is separate from allocating or transferring the weight’s numerical contents.

In this fused-attention example, Q, K, and V are runtime input arguments. They do not populate _weights; the dictionary stays empty unless graph construction adds weights. Weight record · Weight insertion and reuse

A chain represents an ordering dependency between operations.

Consider two operations that access the same mutable buffer:

ops.buffer_store(state, updated)
observed = ops.buffer_load(state)

Suppose updated contains [11, 21]. When the graph executes, the load should observe the completed store. Both operations refer to the same state buffer, so the compiler also needs an explicit ordering relationship.

The graph records that relationship using chain values:

%ch0
  │
  ▼
store updated values into state
  │ produces
  ▼
%ch1
  │
  ▼
load state
  │ produces
  ▼
%ch2

The store consumes the current chain and returns a new chain. The load consumes that new chain. The Python helpers actually update graph.device_chains[device] after staging each operation. Store implementation · Load implementation

A chain has type !mo.chain. Its purpose is dependency tracking; it carries no tensor elements such as the [11, 21] above.

_has_chain_input=False initially means that the graph has no incoming chain argument.

An incoming chain would be an argument supplied by the graph’s caller. That is different from a chain created internally by one of the graph’s operations.

For the attention graph, the declared block arguments are:

B0.arguments = [
    %arg0 : T0,    ← Q
    %arg1 : T0,    ← K
    %arg2 : T0     ← V
]

T0 = !mo.tensor<[32,32],f32,gpu:0>

The constructor initially assumes there is no trailing chain argument, then checks that assumption later. Creating %ch0 internally does not change this flag. Flag initialization and subsequent check

_DeviceChainMap(self) creates an empty map that remembers which Graph owns it.

Its constructor performs essentially:

initialize an empty OrderedDict
self._graph = graph

Therefore, immediately after construction:

G1.device_chains
├─ dictionary contents = {}
└─ _graph → G1

Remembering G1 allows the map to call graph-building helpers if it later needs to create an additional chain block argument.

The map is lazy: accessing a missing device key initializes that device’s chain. For this graph, where _has_chain_input=False, a missing GPU entry is seeded from the current CPU entry:

Request device_chains[gpu:0]
└─ gpu:0 is missing
   └─ _has_chain_input is False
      └─ assign device_chains[gpu:0] = device_chains[cpu:0]

Immediately after the constructor seeds the CPU entry, that would give:

device_chains = {
    cpu:0 → %ch0,
    gpu:0 → %ch0
}

However, the GPU key is added only when something accesses it. Also, if the CPU chain has advanced before that first access, the GPU key receives the current CPU chain at that time. Map constructor and lazy lookup

These map entries describe graph ordering. They are not themselves CUDA streams, worker threads, or work queues.

ChainCreateOp(..., inputs=[]) creates a dependency value with no prerequisites.

The native operation’s contract is: produce a chain that depends on all its inputs.

For example:

%joined = mo.chain.create(%chainA, %chainB)

Meaning:
dependent work may proceed when both prerequisites are satisfied

In the constructor:

%ch0 = mo.chain.create()

inputs = []

There are no preceding values to wait for. Therefore, %ch0 represents an always-ready starting dependency. It provides an initial chain that the first chain-aware operation can consume. Native operation definition

Here, result=ChainType() declares the result type of this ChainCreateOp. It does not add a result to the graph’s function signature or an argument to B0.

The builder inserts that operation into B0 and returns its result wrapper.

The relevant path is:

Graph.__init__()
└─ self._add_op_generated(
       ChainCreateOp,
       result=ChainType(),
       inputs=[]
   )
   ├─ read self._current_block → B0
   ├─ Block._from_cmlir(B0) → native binding wrapper for B0
   ├─ take B0.end
   ├─ create OpBuilder at B0.end
   ├─ construct and insert ChainCreateOp
   │  └─ its result is %ch0 : !mo.chain
   ├─ verify the operation
   └─ Value.from_mlir(%ch0)
      └─ return [_ChainValue wrapping %ch0]

B0.end is the insertion position at the end of the block’s operation list. At this point, it places the chain operation into the graph body.

The helper returns a Python list because operations can have multiple results. This particular operation has one result, so [0] selects %ch0.

Value.from_mlir() sees its mo.ChainType and chooses _ChainValue. The surrounding _ChainValue(...) constructor retains the same underlying MLIR value; it does not insert another operation. Insertion helper · Result-type dispatch · Chain wrapper

_always_ready_chain and the CPU map entry initially refer to the same chain.

These statements:

self._always_ready_chain = ...  # wrapper around %ch0
self._update_chain(self._always_ready_chain)

produce:

G1
├─ _always_ready_chain ───────────┐
│                                ▼
│                       _ChainValue(%ch0)
│                                ▲
└─ device_chains[cpu:0] ──────────┘

_update_chain() simply assigns the supplied value to the CPU entry:

self.device_chains[DeviceRef.CPU()] = new_chain

It does not execute the chain or submit CPU work. Update helper

Later, “advancing a chain” means replacing a map entry with a newly produced SSA value:

Initially:
    _always_ready_chain       → %ch0
    device_chains[gpu:0]      → %ch0

After a GPU buffer store:
    _always_ready_chain       → %ch0
    device_chains[gpu:0]      → %ch1

After the following buffer load:
    _always_ready_chain       → %ch0
    device_chains[gpu:0]      → %ch2

The original %ch0 remains unchanged. The map remembers the latest dependency for subsequent operations.

Keeping _always_ready_chain separately allows operations that are safe to schedule independently of the current device-ordering chain to use the original root. In this checkout, graph-level host/device tensor transfers use it; their tensor operand still supplies the dependency on the data being transferred. Transfer implementation

The final check examines B0’s last argument, not the result of its last operation.

The code reads:

self._graph_body.arguments[-1]

For your attention example:

B0.arguments[-1]
└─ %arg2 / V
   └─ type = T0 / mo.TensorType

_is_chain_value() tests whether that argument’s type is mo.ChainType. V has a tensor type, so the flag remains false.

This check supports internal subgraphs that receive an ordering chain from their caller. Graph.add_subgraph() explicitly appends _ChainType() to their input types. In such a graph, the constructor detects the incoming chain, sets _has_chain_input=True, and uses that incoming value as its CPU ordering seed. The independently created _always_ready_chain remains available. Subgraph construction · Incoming-chain detection

“Three user inputs plus an internal chain OP RESULT” describes two different kinds of SSA value.

Value Defined by Stored as
Q / %arg0 Graph input declaration B0.arguments[0]
K / %arg1 Graph input declaration B0.arguments[1]
V / %arg2 Graph input declaration B0.arguments[2]
%ch0 ChainCreateOp That operation’s results[0]

Using schematic IR, the block now looks like:

B0(%arg0:T0, %arg1:T0, %arg2:T0):
    %ch0 = mo.chain.create()

Its ownership structure is:

B0
├─ arguments: THREE
│  ├─ %arg0 → Q
│  ├─ %arg1 → K
│  └─ %arg2 → V
└─ operations
   └─ ChainCreateOp
      ├─ operands: ZERO
      └─ results: ONE
         └─ %ch0 : !mo.chain

Creating an operation result adds a value inside the computation. Adding a fourth block argument would require an explicit block-argument insertion, such as _add_chain_block_arg(). The constructor’s empty-chain creation does not call that helper. Block-argument insertion helper

For this attention example, the custom call uses the tensor dependencies directly.

The plain ops.custom path records Q/K/V as its operands and returns the attention tensor result. Its schematic body becomes:

B0(%arg0:T0, %arg1:T0, %arg2:T0):
    %ch0 = mo.chain.create()

    %o = mo.custom(%arg0, %arg1, %arg2)
         symbol = "modular_ops::fused_attention_custom"

    mo.output(%o)

The constructor establishes generic chain support even when this particular computation does not consume the root explicitly. The separate ops.inplace_custom path does consume and update a device chain because it supports mutation. Plain custom call · In-place custom call

During compilation, a surviving mo.chain.create lowers to grt.chain.create. During execution, the registered empty-chain primitive can return a reference to the CPU runtime’s existing ready chain. Its dependency payload contains no numerical data; readiness is managed through AsyncRT’s asynchronous value machinery. An unused pure chain operation can also be eliminated during optimization. MO-to-GRT lowering · Runtime primitive · AsyncRT chain payload

Consequently, graph.inputs still returns three symbolic tensors, and your runtime call remains:

output = model.execute(Q, K, V)

The caller supplies Q/K/V. Internal chain dependencies are generated and managed by the graph and runtime. Graph input handling

QnA 2 · Which members of Graph G1 are updated here?

During this part of Graph.__init__()—from weight initialization through the trailing-chain check—four members of G1 are assigned. The chain map and the existing graph body also gain contents.

For this attention example, Q, K, and V are the three tensor inputs. CH0 below names the Python _ChainValue wrapper around the symbolic result %ch0.

Members assigned on G1

Graph member Value after this setup What the assignment does Source
G1._weights {} Creates the empty weight dictionary. Q/K/V are input arguments, so they do not become weight entries. Weight initialization
G1._has_chain_input False Initializes the flag. The subsequent check sees V's tensor type, so the flag remains false. Flag initialization · Trailing-argument check
G1.device_chains _DeviceChainMap(G1), containing DeviceRef.CPU() → CH0 Assigns an initially empty map to G1. _update_chain(CH0) then inserts its CPU entry. The constructor does not create a GPU entry here. Map assignment · CPU entry update
G1._always_ready_chain CH0, wrapping %ch0 : !mo.chain Stores the result of the newly inserted ChainCreateOp. The same wrapper is initially stored in the CPU map entry. Root-chain assignment · Seed the CPU entry

Contents updated through existing references

  1. The map gains an entry: _update_chain(CH0) performs G1.device_chains[DeviceRef.CPU()] = CH0. This changes the map's contents; it keeps the same G1.device_chains map object. Update helper
  2. B0 gains an operation: _add_op_generated() inserts ChainCreateOp at the end of B0's operation list. G1._current_block and G1._graph_body still refer to that same block. G1._mlir_op still wraps the same graph operation, whose region owns B0. The block's three input arguments remain Q/K/V. Existing block references · Insertion point and operation construction

G1 immediately after this setup

G1
├─ _weights = {}
├─ _has_chain_input = False
├─ _always_ready_chain ───────────────→ CH0
├─ device_chains → MAP1
│  ├─ _graph → G1
│  └─ DeviceRef.CPU() ────────────────→ CH0
├─ _current_block ───────────────────→ B0 (same block)
├─ _graph_body ──────────────────────→ B0 (same block)
└─ _mlir_op → mo.graph → region 0 → B0
   ├─ arguments: [Q, K, V]                  (unchanged)
   └─ operations: [ChainCreateOp]            (new operation)
      └─ results[0]: %ch0 : !mo.chain

CH0 = the _ChainValue wrapper around %ch0
MAP1 has no DeviceRef.GPU(0) entry at this point.

In _DeviceChainMap.__init__(), self is MAP1. Its self._graph = graph creates G1.device_chains._graph → G1; it does not create a G1._graph member. Also, G1.always_ready_chain is a property that reads _always_ready_chain, rather than a second stored member. Map's Graph reference · Ready-chain property

Later changes described in the original Q&A

A first lookup of a missing GPU key can add G1.device_chains[DeviceRef.GPU(0)]. Later chain-aware operations replace that device's map entry with their new result chain. These are updates to the existing map; _always_ready_chain keeps the original root. Lazy device lookup · Buffer-store chain update

For an internal subgraph whose last input is a chain, the check additionally sets _has_chain_input=True and replaces the CPU map entry with that incoming chain. The independent _always_ready_chain remains available. Your Q/K/V attention graph keeps _has_chain_input=False. Incoming-chain branch

QnA 3 · Does graph.device_chains[device] get updated in this setup?

Yes—the CPU entry is updated here, when the constructor calls:

Graph.__init__()
└─ self._update_chain(self._always_ready_chain)
   └─ self.device_chains[DeviceRef.CPU()] = new_chain

_update_chain() explicitly writes the CPU key, regardless of whether Q/K/V belong to a GPU. See the implementation.

After your quoted section:

G1.device_chains
└─ cpu:0 → _ChainValue wrapping %ch0

G1._always_ready_chain → that same wrapper

%ch0 is the result of ChainCreateOp. The map stores its Python wrapper.

If device means gpu:0, that entry is added later. Because this graph has _has_chain_input=False, the first lookup:

graph.device_chains[DeviceRef.GPU(0)]

automatically inserts the GPU key, seeded from the current CPU chain. See the lazy lookup.

The final V-argument check leaves _has_chain_input=False and the CPU entry pointing to %ch0.
